A Complete Axiomatisation for Quantifier-Free Separation Logic
Stéphane Demri \rsuper a, Étienne Lozes \rsuper b, Alessio Mansutti \rsuper a

Abstract
. We present the first complete axiomatisation for quantifier-free separation logic. The logic is equipped with the standard concrete heaplet semantics and the proof system has no external feature such as nominals/labels. It is not possible to rely completely on proof systems for Boolean BI as the concrete semantics needs to be taken into account. Therefore, we present the first internal Hilbert-style axiomatisation for quantifier-free separation logic. The calculus is divided in three parts: the axiomatisation of core formulae where Boolean combinations of core formulae capture the expressivity of the whole logic, axioms and inference rules to simulate a bottom-up elimination of separating connectives, and finally structural axioms and inference rules from propositional calculus and Boolean BI with the magic wand.

\lsuper

*This is the long version of the first part of
[
DLM20
]
.

†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 17, Issue 3, Paper 17

1.
Introduction

The virtue of axiomatising program logics

Designing a Hilbert-style axiomatisation for your favourite logic is usually quite challenging.
This does not lead necessarily to optimal decision procedures, but the completeness proof usually provides essential
insights to better understand the logic at hand. That is why many logics related to program verification
have been axiomatised, often requiring non-trivial completeness proofs.
By way of example, there are axiomatisations for
the linear-time
\mu
-calculus
[
Kai95
,
Dou17
]
,
the modal
\mu
-calculus
[
Wal00
]

or for
the alternating-time temporal logic ATL
[
GvD06
]
,
the full computation tree logic CTL
∗

[
Rey01
]
,
for probabilistic extensions of
\mu
-calculus
[
LMX16
]

or for a coalgebraic generalisation
[
SV18
]
.
Concerning the separation logics that extend Hoare-Floyd logic
to verify programs with mutable data structures
(see e.g.
[
OP99
,
Rey02
,
IO01
,
O’H12
,
PSO18
]
),
a Hilbert-style axiomatisation of Boolean BI has been introduced in
[
GLW06
]
,
but remained at the abstract level of Boolean BI. More recently, HyBBI
[
BV14
]
,
a hybrid version of Boolean BI has been introduced in order
to axiomatise various classes of abstract separation logics; HyBBI
naturally considers classes of abstract models (typically preordered partial monoids) but it does not fit
exactly the heaplet semantics of separation logics. Furthermore, the addition of nominals (in the sense
of hybrid modal logics, see e.g.
[
ABM01
]
) extends substantially the object language. Other frameworks
to axiomatise classes of abstract separation logics can be found
in
[
DP18
,
Doc19
]
and in
[
HCGT18
]
, respectively with labelled tableaux calculi
and with sequent-style proof systems.

Our motivations

Since the birth of separation logics, there has been a lot of interest in the study of
decidability and computational complexity issues,
see e.g.
[
COY01
,
BDL09
,
BIP10
,
CHO+11
,
DGLWM17
,
BK18
,
DLM18a
,
Man18
,
Man20
]
,
and comparatively less attention to the design of proof systems, and even less with the puristic approach
that consists in discarding any external feature such as nominals or labels in the calculi.
The well-known advantages of such an approach include an exhaustive understanding of the expressive power of
the logic and discarding the use of any external artifact referring to semantical objects.
For instance, a tableaux calculus with labels for quantifier-free separation logic is designed in
[
GM10
]
,
whereas Hilbert-style calculi for abstract separation logics with nominals are defined in
[
BV14
]
.
Similarly, display calculi for bunched logics are provided in
[
Bro12
]
but such calculi extend
Gentzen-style proof systems by allowing new structural connectives, which provides an elegant means to simulate labels.
In this paper, we advocate a puristic approach and aim at designing a Hilbert-style proof system
for quantifier-free
separation logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
(which includes the separating conjunction
\ast
and implication
\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}
, as well as all
Boolean connectives) and more generally for other separation logics, while remaining within the very logical
language (see the second part of
[
DLM20
]
).
1
1

1

We aim at defining
internal
calculi according to the terminology from
the Workshop on External and Internal Calculi for Non-Classical Logics, FLOC’18, Oxford,

http://weic2018.loria.fr
.

Consequently, in this work, we only focus on axiomatising separation logics,
and we have no claim for practical applications in the field of program verification with separation logics.
Aiming at internal calculi is a non-trivial
task as the general frameworks for abstract separation logics make use of labels, see e.g.
[
DP18
,
HCGT18
]
.
We cannot rely on label-free calculi for BI, see e.g.
[
Pym02
,
GLW06
]
, as separation logics are usually understood
as Boolean BI interpreted on models of heap memory and therefore require calculi that cannot abstract as much as
it is the case for Boolean BI. Finally, there are many translations from separation logics into
logics or theories,
see e.g.
[
CGH05
,
PWZ13
,
BDL12
,
RISK16
]
.
However, completeness cannot in general be inherited by sublogics as the proof system should
only use the sublogic and therefore the axiomatisation of sublogics may lead
to different methods.
A more detailed discussion
about the related work can be found in Section
7
.

Our contribution

We propose a modular axiomatisation of quantifier-free separation logic, starting with a complete
axiomatisation of a Boolean algebra of core formulae, and incrementally adding support for the
spatial connectives: the separating conjunction and the separating implication (a.k.a. the magic wand).
The same approach could be followed for other fragments of separation logic, as we did in the conference
version of this paper
[
DLM20
]
(see also a similar approach in
[
DFM19
]
).
Thus, our approach can be considered with the broader perspective of a generic method for axiomatising
separation logics. Let us be a bit more precise.

In Section
3
, we present the first Hilbert-style proof system
for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
that uses axiom schemas and rules involving only formulae of this logic.
We mainly introduce our approach and present the notations
that are used throughout the paper.
Each formula of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
is equivalent to a Boolean combination of
core formulae
:
simple formulae of the logic expressing elementary properties
about the models
[
Loz04b
]
.
Though core formulae (also called
test formulae
) have been handy in several occasions for
establishing complexity results for separation logics,
see e.g.
[
BDL09
,
DLM18a
,
Man18
,
EIP19
]
,
in the paper, these formulae are instrumental for the axiomatisation.
Indeed, the axiomatisation of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
is designed starting from
an axiomatisation of Boolean combinations of core formulae (introduced in Section
4
),
and adding axioms and rules that allow to syntactically transform every formula of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
into such Boolean combinations.
This transformation is introduced in Section
5
and in Section
6
:
the former section shows how to eliminate the separating conjunction
\ast
,
whereas the latter one treat the separating implication
\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}
.
Schematically, for a valid formula
\varphi
, we conclude
\vdash\varphi
from

\vdash\varphi^{\prime}
and
\vdash\varphi^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\varphi
, where

\varphi^{\prime}
is a Boolean combination of core formulae.
Our methodology leads to a calculus that is divided in three parts: (1) the axiomatisation
of Boolean combinations of core formulae,
(2) axioms and inference rules to simulate a bottom-up elimination of the separating conjunction,
and (3) axioms and inference rules to simulate a bottom-up elimination of the magic wand.
Such an approach that consists in first axiomatising a syntactic fragment of the whole logic (in our case, the core formulae),
is best described in
[
Dou17
]

(see also
[
Wal00
,
vB11
,
WC13
,
Lüc18
,
DFM19
]
).
Section
7
compares works from the literature with our contribution, either for
separation logics (abstract versions, fragments, etc.) or for knowledge logics for which the axiomatisation has been performed
by using a reduction to a strict syntactic fragment though expressively complete.

This paper is the complete version of the first part of
[
DLM20
]
dedicated
to quantifier-free separation logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
. The complete version of the second part of
[
DLM20
]

dedicated to the new separation logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\exists{:}{\rightsquigarrow}\mathsf{)}
is too long to be included in the present document.
A technical appendix contains syntactic derivations omitted from the body of the paper.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Quantifier-free separation logic

We present the quantifier-free separation logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
, that includes standard features such as
the separating conjunction
\ast
, the separating implication
\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}

and closure under Boolean connectives.
Let
\mathsf{VAR}=\{\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y},\ldots\}
be a countably infinite set of
program variables
.
The formulae
\varphi
of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
and its atomic formulae

\pi
are built from the grammars below where
\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}\in\mathsf{VAR}
.

\pi::=\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}\ \mid\ \mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\ \mid\ \mathtt{emp}\qquad\qquad\varphi::=\pi\ \mid\ \neg\varphi\ \mid\ \varphi\wedge\varphi\ \mid\ \varphi\ast\varphi\ \mid\ \varphi\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\varphi.

The connectives
\Rightarrow
,
\Leftrightarrow
and
\vee
are defined as usually.
In the heaplet semantics, the formulae of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
are interpreted on
memory states
that are pairs

(s,h)
where

{s:\mathsf{VAR}\rightarrow\mathsf{LOC}}
is
a variable valuation (the
store
) from the set of program variables to a
countably infinite set of
locations

\mathsf{LOC}=\{\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots\}
, whereas

h:\mathsf{LOC}\to_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{LOC}
is a partial function with finite domain (the
heap
).
We write
{\rm dom}(h)
to denote its domain and
{\rm ran}(h)
to denote its range.
A
memory cell
of
h
is understood as a pair of locations
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})

such that
\ell\in{\rm dom}(h)
and
\ell^{\prime}=h(\ell)
.
As usual, the heaps
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are said to be
disjoint
, written
h_{1}\sharp h_{2}
,
if
{{\rm dom}(h_{1})\cap{\rm dom}(h_{2})=\emptyset}
; when this holds, we write
h_{1}+h_{2}
to denote the heap
corresponding to the disjoint union of the graphs of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, hence
{\rm dom}(h_{1}+h_{2})={\rm dom}(h_{1})\uplus{\rm dom}(h_{2})
.
When the domains of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are not disjoint, the composition
h_{1}+h_{2}
is not defined.
Moreover, we write
h^{\prime}\sqsubseteq h
to denote that
{\rm dom}(h^{\prime})\subseteq{\rm dom}(h)
and for all locations

\ell\in{\rm dom}(h^{\prime})
, we have
h^{\prime}(\ell)=h(\ell)
.
If
h^{\prime}\sqsubseteq h
then
h^{\prime}
is said to be a
subheap
of
h
.
The satisfaction relation
\models
is defined as follows
(we omit standard clauses for the Boolean connectives
\neg
and
\wedge
):

(s,h)\models\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{\;\Leftrightarrow\;}}

s(\mathtt{x})=s(\mathtt{y})
,

(s,h)\models\mathtt{emp}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{\;\Leftrightarrow\;}}

{\rm dom}(h)=\emptyset
,

(s,h)\models\mathtt{x}{\hookrightarrow}\mathtt{y}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{\;\Leftrightarrow\;}}

s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h)
and
h(s(\mathtt{x}))=s(\mathtt{y})
,

(s,h)\models\varphi_{1}\ast\varphi_{2}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{\;\Leftrightarrow\;}}

there are

h_{1},h_{2}
such that

h_{1}\sharp h_{2}
,
(h_{1}+h_{2})=h
,

(s,h_{1})\models\varphi_{1}
and
(s,h_{2})\models\varphi_{2}
,

(s,h)\models\varphi_{1}\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\varphi_{2}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{\;\Leftrightarrow\;}}

for all
h_{1}
such that

h_{1}\sharp h
and
(s,h_{1})\models\varphi_{1}
,

we have
(s,h+h_{1})\models\varphi_{2}
.

We denote with
\bot
the contradiction
\mathtt{x}\neq\mathtt{x}
, and with
\top
its negation
\neg\bot
.
The septraction operator
\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}
(kind of dual of
\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}
), defined
by
\varphi\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\neg(\varphi\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\neg\psi)
,
has the following semantics:

(s,h)\models\varphi\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}\psi

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{\;\Leftrightarrow\;}}
there is a heap
h^{\prime}
such that

h\sharp h^{\prime}
,
(s,h^{\prime})\models\varphi
, and

(s,h+h^{\prime})\models\psi
.

We adopt the standard precedence between classical connectives, and extend it for the connectives of separation logic as follows:
\{\lnot\}>\{\land,\lor,\ast\}>\{\Rightarrow,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*},\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}\}>\{\Leftrightarrow\}
.
Notice that the separating conjunction
\ast
has a higher precedence than the separating implication
\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}
, and it has the same precedence as the (classical) conjunction
\land
. For instance,
\varphi\ast\psi\Rightarrow\chi
and
\lnot\varphi\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\psi\ast\psi

stand for
(\varphi\ast\psi)\Rightarrow\chi
and
(\lnot\varphi)\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}(\psi\ast\psi)
, respectively.

A formula
\varphi
is
valid
if
(s,h)\models\varphi
for all
memory states
(s,h)
(and we write
\models\varphi
).
For a complete description of separation logic, see e.g.
[
Rey02
]
.
Given a set of formulae
\Gamma
, we write
\Gamma\models\varphi
(semantical entailment)
whenever
(s,h)\models\varphi
holds
for every memory state
(s,h)
satisfying every formula in
\Gamma
.

It is worth noting that quantifier-free
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
axiomatised in the paper admits a
PSpace
-complete validity problem,
see e.g.
[
COY01
]
, and should not be confused with propositional separation logic with the stack-heap models
shown undecidable in
[
BK14
, Corollary 5.1]

(see also
[
DD15
, Section 4]
), in which there are propositional variables interpreted by sets of
memory states.

2.2.
Core formulae

We introduce the following well-known shortcuts, that play an important role in the sequel.
Let
\mathtt{x}\in\mathsf{VAR}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
.

Shortcut:

Definition:

Semantics:

\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

(\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{x})\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\perp

(s,h)\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
iff
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h)

\mathtt{size}\geq\beta

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\begin{cases}\top&\text{if}~\beta=0\\
\lnot\mathtt{emp}&\text{if}~\beta=1\\
\lnot\mathtt{emp}\ast\mathtt{size}\geq\beta{-}1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(s,h)\models\mathtt{size}\geq\beta
iff
{\rm card}({\rm dom}(h))\geq\beta

We use
\mathtt{size}{=}\beta
as a shorthand for
\mathtt{size}{\geq}\beta\land\lnot\mathtt{size}{\geq}\beta{+}1
.
We also write
{\rm card}(X)
to denote the cardinality of the set
X
.

The
core formulae
are expressions of the form

\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}
,

\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
,

\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
and

\mathtt{size}\geq\beta
,
where
\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}\in\mathsf{VAR}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
.
As we can see, the core formulae are simple
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
formulae.
It is well-known, see e.g.
[
Yan01
,
Loz04a
]
, that these formulae capture essential properties of the memory states.
In particular, every formula of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
is logically equivalent to a Boolean combination of
core formulae
[
Loz04a
]
.

As a simple but crucial insight, since the core formulae are formulae of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
, we can
freely use them to help us defining the proof system for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
, and preventing us from going outside the original language.
Having this in mind, the resulting proof system is Hilbert-style and completely internal
(the formal definition of these types of systems is recalled below).

Given
\mathtt{X}\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{VAR}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,
we define
\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
as the set

\{\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y},\ \mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}),\ \mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y},\ \mathtt{size}\geq\beta\mid\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}\in\mathtt{X},\ \beta\in[0,\alpha]\}.

\mathsf{Bool}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha))
is defined as the set of Boolean
combinations of formulae from
\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
, whereas

\mathsf{Conj}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha))
is the set of conjunctions of literals built upon
\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
.
As usual, a
literal
is understood as a core formula or its negation.
Let
{\varphi=L_{1}\land\dots\land L_{n}}\in\mathsf{Conj}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha))
be a conjunction of literals
L_{1},\dots,L_{n}
.
We write
\mathsf{Lt}(\varphi)
for
\{L_{1},\dots,L_{n}\}
.
In forthcoming developments, we are interested in the maximum
\beta
(if any) of formulae of the form
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta
occurring positively in a conjunction of literals, if any.
For this reason, we write
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)
for
{\max(\{\beta\in\mathbb{N}\mid\mathtt{size}\geq\beta\in\mathsf{Lt}(\varphi)\}\cup\{0\})}
.
For instance,
given
\varphi={\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\mathtt{size}\geq 2\land\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq 4}
,
we have

\mathsf{Lt}(\varphi)=\{\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}),\mathtt{size}\geq 2,\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq 4\}
,
and
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)=2
.
Given two conjunctions of literals
\varphi\in\mathsf{Conj}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha_{1}))
and
\psi\in\mathsf{Conj}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha_{2}))
,

\psi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
stands for
\mathsf{Lt}(\psi)\subseteq\mathsf{Lt}(\varphi)
.
Finally, we introduce a few more shortcuts and we write

•

\chi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\{\varphi\mathbin{|}\psi\}
for  “
\chi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
or
\chi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi
”,

•

\{\varphi\mathbin{|}\psi\}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\chi
for  “
\varphi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\chi
or
\psi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\chi
”.

•

\chi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\{\varphi\mathbin{;}\psi\}
for  “
\chi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
and
\chi\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi
”,

Given a finite set of formulae
\Gamma=\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}\}
,
we write
\bigwedge\Gamma
as a shorthand for
\varphi_{1}\land\dots\land\varphi_{n}
.
Similarly,

\Asterisk\Gamma
stands for
\varphi_{1}\ast\dots\ast\varphi_{n}
.
It is important to notice that, similarly to the classical conjunction, the separating conjunction
\ast
is associative and commutative
(see the axioms
(

A
∗

9

)
and
(

A
∗

7

)
in Figure
1
), and therefore
the semantics of
\Asterisk\Gamma
is uniquely defined,
regardless of the choice of ordering for
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}
.

2.3.
Hilbert-style proof systems

A
Hilbert-style proof system

\mathcal{H}
is defined as a set of
tuples

((\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}),\Psi)
with
n\geq 0
,
where
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},\Psi
are
formula schemata
(a.k.a

axiom schemata
).
When
n\geq 1
,
((\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}),\Psi)
is called an
inference rule
,
otherwise it is an
axiom
. As usual, formula schemata generalise the notion of formulae by allowing metavariables
for formulae (typically
\varphi,\psi,\chi
), for program variables (typically
\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y},\mathtt{z}
)
or for any type of syntactic objects in formulae, depending on the context.
The set of formulae
derivable
from

\mathcal{H}
is the least set
S
such that for all

((\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}),\Psi)\in\mathcal{H}
and for all
substitutions
\sigma
, if

\Phi_{1}\sigma,\dots,\Phi_{n}\sigma\in S

then
\Psi\sigma\in S
.
We write
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}}\varphi
if
\varphi
is
derivable from
\mathcal{H}
.
A proof system
\mathcal{H}

is
sound
if all derivable formulae are valid.
\mathcal{H}

is
complete
if
all valid formulae are derivable.
We say that
\mathcal{H}
is
adequate
whenever it is both sound and complete.
Lastly,
\mathcal{H}
is
strongly complete

whenever for all sets of formulae
\Gamma
and formulae
\varphi
,
we have
\Gamma\models\varphi
(semantical entailment) if and only if

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}\cup\Gamma}\varphi
.

Interestingly enough, there is no strongly complete proof system
for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
, as strong completeness implies compactness
and separation logic is not compact.
Indeed, the set
{\{\mathtt{size}\geq\beta\mid\beta\in\mathbb{N}\}}
is unsatisfiable, as heaps have finite domains, but all finite subsets of it
are satisfiable.
Even for the weaker notion of completeness, deriving an Hilbert-style axiomatisation for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
remains challenging.
Indeed, the satisfiability problem for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
reduces to its validity problem,
making
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
an unusual logic from a proof-theoretical point of view.
Let us develop a bit further this point.

Let
\varphi
be a formula built over program variables in
\mathtt{X}\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{VAR}
, and let
\approx
be an equivalence relation on
\mathtt{X}
.
The formula

\psi_{\approx}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\mathtt{emp}\land\bigwedge_{\mathtt{x}\approx\mathtt{y}}\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}\land\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{x}\not\approx\mathtt{y}\end{subarray}}\mathtt{x}\neq\mathtt{y})\Rightarrow(\varphi\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}\top)

can be shown to be valid iff
for every store
s
agreeing on
\approx
, there is a heap
h
such that
(s,h)\models\varphi
.
It is known
that for all stores
s,s^{\prime}
agreeing on
\approx
, and every heap
h
,
the memory states
(s,h)
and
(s^{\prime},h)

satisfy the same set of formulae having variables from
\mathtt{X}
.
Since the antecedent of
\psi_{\approx}
is satisfiable, we conclude that

\psi_{\approx}
is valid iff there are a store
s
agreeing on
\approx
and a heap
h
such that

(s,h)\models\varphi
.
To check whether
\varphi
is satisfiable, it is sufficient to find an equivalence relation
\approx
on
\mathtt{X}
such that

\psi_{\approx}
is valid. As the number of equivalence relations on
\mathtt{X}
is finite, we obtain a Turing reduction
from satisfiability to validity.
Consequently, it is not possible to define sound and complete axiom systems for any extension of

\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
admitting an undecidable validity problem
(as long as there is a reduction from satisfiability to validity, as above).
A good example is
the logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*},\mathtt{ls}\mathsf{)}

[
DLM18b
]
(extension of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
with the well-known list-segment predicate
\mathtt{ls}
); see also the first-order
separation logic in
[
BDL12
]
.
Indeed, to obtain a sound and complete axiom system, the validity problem has to be recursively enumerable (r.e.).
However, this would imply that the satisfiability problem is also r.e.. As
a formula

\varphi
is not valid if and only if

\lnot\varphi
is satisfiable, we then conclude that the set of valid formulae is recursive,
hence decidable, a contradiction.

3.
Hilbert-style proof system for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}

\ast
-Intro
:

\text{}\frac{\displaystyle\varphi\Rightarrow\chi}{\displaystyle\varphi\ast\psi\Rightarrow\chi\ast\psi}{}

\ast
-Adj
:

\text{}\frac{\displaystyle\varphi\ast\psi\Rightarrow\chi}{\displaystyle\varphi\Rightarrow(\psi\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\chi)}{}

\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}
-Adj
:

\text{}\frac{\displaystyle\varphi\Rightarrow(\psi\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\chi)}{\displaystyle\varphi\ast\psi\Rightarrow\chi}{}

(axioms and modus ponens from propositional calculus are omitted)

Figure 1.
The proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
.

In Figure
1
, we present the proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})

that shall be shown to be sound and complete for quantifier-free separation logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
.

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
and all the subsequent fragments of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
contain
the axiom schemata and modus ponens for the propositional calculus (we omit these rules
in the presentation). In the axioms
(

A
∗

18

)
,
(

A

-
⁣
∗

21

)
and
(

A

-
⁣
∗

25

)
,
the notation
\varphi\!\!\ \blacktriangleleft\!\!\!\left[\mathcal{B}\right]

refers to the axiom schema
\varphi
assuming that the Boolean condition
\mathcal{B}
holds.
We highlight the fact that, in these three axioms,
\mathcal{B}
is a simple syntactical condition.
In the axiom
(

A
∗

28

)
,
a\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}b
, where
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, stands for
\max(0,a-b)
.

Though the full proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
is presented quite early in the paper,
its final design remains the outcome of a refined analysis on principles
behind
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
tautologies.
Fortunately, we do not start from scratch as the calculus must contain
the axioms and rules from the Hilbert-style proof system for Boolean BI
[
GLW06
]
.
At first glance the system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
may seem quite arbitrary,
but the role of the different axioms shall become clearer during the paper.
In designing the system, we tried to define axioms that are as simple as possible,
which helps highlighting the most fundamental properties of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
.
Note that we have not formally proved that our proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})

is minimal (though we have tried our best to have a small amount of small axioms).
Such an investigation would be out of the scope of the paper, mainly for lack of space.
The standard way to proceed would be to design models different from memory states
and to establish that all axioms but one are valid (which would prove that this axiom is
needed when all the other axioms are present).

We insist: the core formulae in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
should be understood as mere abbreviations,
which makes all the axioms in Figure
1
belong to the original language of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
.
In order to show the completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
,
we first establish the completeness for subsystems of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
,
with respect to syntactical fragments of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
. In particular, we consider

•

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
: an adequate proof system for the propositional logic of core formulae (see Figure
4
),

•

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
: an extension of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
that is adequate for the logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}
,
i.e. the logic obtained from
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
by removing the separating implication
\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}
at the price of adding the formula
\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
(see Figure
5
).

•

The full
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
, which can be seen as an extension of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
that allows to reason about
the separating implication (see Figure
7
).

For the completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
, we add intermediate axioms that reveal to be useless when the full proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
is considered, as they become derivable. By convention,
the axioms whose name is of the form
A^{?}_{i}
are axioms that remain in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})

(see Figure
1
)
whereas those named
I^{?}_{i}
are intermediate axioms that are instrumental for the proof of completeness of a subsystem among
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)

(and therefore none of them occur in Figure
1
).
The numbering of the axioms in Figure
1

is not consecutive, as intermediate axioms shall be placed within the holes.
It is worth noting that the axiom
(

A
∗

16

)
had an intermediate status in
[
DLM20
]

but we realised that actually this axiom does need to be considered as a first-class axiom in the proof system

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
.

The choice of introducing
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
naturally follows from the
main steps required for the completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
.
In particular, the main “task” of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
is to produce a bottom-up elimination of the separating conjunction
\ast
, at the price of introducing Boolean combinations of core formulae, which can be proved valid thanks to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
. Similarly, the axioms and rules added to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
to define
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
are dedicated to perform a bottom-up elimination of the separating implication.
A merit of this methodology is that only the completeness of the calculus
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}

is proved using the standard countermodel method.
The additional steps required to prove the completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
are (almost) completely syntactical. For instance,
to show the completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
, we consider arbitrary
Boolean combinations of core formulae
\varphi
and
\psi
,
and exhibiting a Boolean combination of core formulae
\chi
such that
\varphi\ast\psi\Leftrightarrow\chi
is valid. We show that this validity can be
syntactically
proved within
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
, and then rely on the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
is complete for Boolean combination of core formulae to deduce that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
is complete for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}
.

Along the paper, we shall have the opportunity to explain the intuition between the axioms and rules.
Below, we provide a few hints. The axioms
(

A
C

1

)
–
(

A
C

7

)
deal with the core formulae
and are quite immediate to grasp. More interestingly, whereas the axioms
(

A
∗

7

)
–
(

A
∗

13

)

are quite general about separating conjunction and are inherited from Boolean BI, the axioms
(

A
∗

18

)
–
(

A
∗

30

)

state how separating conjunction behaves with the core formulae. As for Boolean combinations of core formulae
involved in the axioms
(

A
C

1

)
–
(

A
C

7

)
, these axioms
(

A
∗

18

)
–
(

A
∗

30

)

are also not difficult
to understand.
Besides, the inference rules
1
and
1
simply
reflect that separating conjunction and separating implication are adjoint operators,
and are taken from Boolean BI, see e.g.
[
GLW06
]
.
The axioms
(

A

-
⁣
∗

21

)
–
(

A

-
⁣
∗

25

)
dedicated to the interaction between the separating
implication and core formulae are expressed with the help of the septraction operator
\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}
to ease the understanding
but as well-known, septraction is defined with the help of the separating implication and Boolean negation.
For instance, the axiom
(

A

-
⁣
∗

23

)
states that it is always possible to add some one-memory-cell
heap
h^{\prime}
to some heap
h
while none of the variables from a finite set
\mathtt{X}
is allocated
in
h^{\prime}
. This natural property in our framework would not hold in general if
\mathsf{LOC}
were
not infinite. Obviously, the septraction
\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}
is also understood as an abbreviation.

As a sanity check, we show that the proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
is sound with respect
to
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
. The proof does not pose any specific difficulty (as usual with most soundness proofs)
but this is the opportunity for the reader to further get familiar with the axioms and rules
from
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
.

Lemma 1
.

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
is sound.

The validity of the axioms
(

A
C

1

)
,
(

A
C

3

)
,
(

A
C

5

)
and
(

A
C

7

)
is
straightforward. Moreover, the validity of the axioms
(

A
∗

7

)
,
(

A
∗

9

)
and
(

A
∗

13

)

and the three inference rules (
1
,
1
and
1
) is inherited from Boolean BI
(see
[
BV14
]
and
[
GLW06
, Section 2]
).
Below, we show the validity of the remaining axioms, thus proving Lemma
1
.

Proof 3.1

(Validity of the axiom
(

A
∗

16

)
)
.

Let us show that
(\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\ast\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))
is not satisfiable.
Ad absurdum, suppose there is a memory state
(s,h)
such that

(s,h)\models(\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\ast\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))
.
By definition of
\models
, there are

h_{1},h_{2}
such that

h_{1}\bot h_{2}
,
(h_{1}+h_{2})=h
,

(s,h_{1})\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
and
(s,h_{2})\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.
Thus,
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h_{1})
and
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h_{2})
,
which leads to a contradiction with
h_{1}\bot h_{2}
.

Proof 3.2

(Validity of the axiom
(

A
∗

18

)
)
.

The proof of the validity of every instantiation of
(

A
∗

18

)
is similar (and quite easy),
therefore we show just the case with
\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\ast\top\Rightarrow\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
.
Suppose
(s,h)\models\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\ast\top
. Then, there is a subheap
h_{1}\sqsubseteq h
such that
(s,h_{1})\models\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
.
Hence,
h_{1}(s(\mathtt{x}))=s(\mathtt{y})
.
As
h_{1}\sqsubseteq h
, we obtain
h(s(\mathtt{x}))=s(\mathtt{y})
,
which implies
(s,h)\models\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
.

Proof 3.3

(Validity of the axiom
(

A
∗

20

)
)
.

Suppose
(s,h)\models\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\ast\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
. Then, there are
two disjoint heaps
h_{1},h_{2}
such that
h=h_{1}+h_{2}
,
(s,h_{1})\models\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
and
(s,h_{2})\models\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.
Then
s(\mathtt{x})\not\in{\rm dom}(h_{1})
and
s(\mathtt{x})\not\in{\rm dom}(h_{2})
.
Since
h=h_{1}+h_{2}
,
{\rm dom}(h)={\rm dom}(h_{1})\cup{\rm dom}(h_{2})
and therefore

s(\mathtt{x})\not\in{\rm dom}(h)
.
We conclude that
(s,h)\models\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.

Proof 3.4

(Validity of the axiom
(

A
∗

22

)
)
.

Suppose
(s,h)\models(\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\lnot\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y})\ast\top
.
Then there is a subheap
h_{1}\sqsubseteq h
such that
(s,h_{1})\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\lnot\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
.
Hence,
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h_{1})
and
h_{1}(s(\mathtt{x}))\neq s(\mathtt{y})
.
As
h_{1}\sqsubseteq h
, we obtain
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h)
and
h(s(\mathtt{x}))\neq s(\mathtt{y})
which by definition implies
(s,h)\models\lnot\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
.

Proof 3.5

(Validity of the axiom
(

A
∗

24

)
)
.

Suppose
(s,h)\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.
Let
h_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{s(\mathtt{x})\mapsto h(s(\mathtt{x}))\}

As
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h)
,
h_{1}\sqsubseteq h
and

(s,h_{1})\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\mathtt{size}=1
.
We define
h_{2}
as the unique heap such that
h_{2}+h_{1}=h
.
As
(s,h_{2})\models\top
,
we have
(s,h)\models(\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\mathtt{size}=1)\ast\top
.

The proof of axiom
(

A
∗

26

)
is similar to the one of
(

A
∗

24

)
, and hence omitted herein.

Proof 3.6

(Validity of the axiom
(

A
∗

28

)
)
.

Suppose
(s,h)\models\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{1}\ast\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{2}
, where
{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\geq 0}
.
Since
\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq 0
is not satisfiable, this implies that necessarily
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\geq 1
.
Hence, the axiom
(

A
∗

28

)
is trivially valid when
\beta_{1}=0
or
\beta_{2}=0
.
In the sequel,
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\geq 1
.
Then, there are heaps
h_{1},h_{2}
such that
h_{1}\sharp h_{2}
,
h_{1}+h_{2}=h
,

(s,h_{1})\models\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{1}
and
{(s,h_{2})\models\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{2}}
.
By definition,
{\rm card}({\rm dom}(h_{1}))\leq\beta_{1}-1
and
{\rm card}({\rm dom}(h_{2}))\leq\beta_{2}-1
.
Since
{\rm dom}(h)={\rm dom}(h_{1})\cup{\rm dom}(h_{2})
, we obtain
{\rm card}({\rm dom}(h))\leq\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-2
, which implies

(s,h)\models\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}1
.

Proof 3.7

(Validity of the axiom
(

A
∗

30

)
)
.

Suppose
(s,h)\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{y})\land\mathtt{x}\neq\mathtt{y}
.
By definition,

s(\mathtt{x})\neq s(\mathtt{y})
, and
s(\mathtt{x}),s(\mathtt{y})\in{\rm dom}(h)
. Hence,
{\rm card}({\rm dom}(h))\geq 2
, and
(s,h)\models\mathtt{size}\geq 2
.

Proof 3.8

(Validity of the axiom
(

A

-
⁣
∗

21

)
)
.

Let
\mathtt{X}\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{VAR}
and
(s,h)
be a memory state.
Let
h_{1}
be a heap of size one such that
h_{1}(\ell)=\ell
for some

\ell\not\in{\rm dom}(h)\cup s(\mathtt{X})
. We write
s(\mathtt{X})
to denote
the set
\{s(\mathtt{x})\mid\mathtt{x}\in\mathtt{X}\}
.
Trivially
(s,h_{1})\models\mathtt{size}=1\land\bigwedge_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathtt{X}}\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.
Moreover
h_{1}\sharp h
holds, hence
h_{1}+h_{2}
is defined and
(s,h+h_{1})\models\top
.
Then,
(s,h)\models(\mathtt{size}=1\land\bigwedge_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathtt{X}}\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}\top
.

Proof 3.9

(Validity of the axiom
(

A

-
⁣
∗

23

)
)
.

Suppose
(s,h)\models\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.
Let
h_{1}
be the heap of size one such that
h_{1}(s(\mathtt{x}))=s(\mathtt{y})
.
Trivially,
(s,h_{1})\models\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\land\mathtt{size}=1
.
Moreover, as
s(\mathtt{x})\not\in{\rm dom}(h)
,
h_{1}\sharp h
holds. Therefore,
h_{1}+h

is defined, and
(s,h+h_{1})\models\top
.
Then,
(s,h)\models(\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\land\mathtt{size}=1)\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}\top
.

Proof 3.10

(Validity of the axiom
(

A

-
⁣
∗

25

)
)
.

Suppose
(s,h)\models\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.
Let
\mathtt{X}\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{VAR}
and
h_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{s(\mathtt{x})\mapsto\ell\}
, where
\ell\not\in s(\mathtt{X})
.
Hence,
{(s,h_{1})\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\mathtt{size}=1}\land\bigwedge_{\mathtt{y}\in\mathtt{X}}\lnot\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
.
Since
s(\mathtt{x})\not\in{\rm dom}(h)
,

h_{1}\sharp h
.
Therefore, the heap
h+h_{1}
is defined and
(s,h+h_{1})\models\top
.
Then,
(s,h)\models(\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\mathtt{size}=1\land\bigwedge_{\mathtt{y}\in\mathtt{X}}\lnot\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y})\mathbin{{-\mkern-8.0mu{\raisebox{0.6pt}{$\scriptstyle{\circledast}$}}}}\top
.

Figure 2.
A proof of
\mathtt{emp}\Rightarrow\big((\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\mathtt{size}=1)\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq 2\big)
.

To further familiarise with the axioms and the rules of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
, in Figure
1
,
we present a proof of

\mathtt{emp}\Rightarrow\big(\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\mathtt{size}=1\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq 2\big)
.
In the proof, a line “
j\,\mid\,\chi\ \ A,i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
”
states that
\chi
is a theorem denoted by the index
j
and derivable by the axiom or the rule
A
.
If
A
is a rule, the indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}<j
denote the theorems used as premises in order to derive
\chi
.
When a formula is obtained as a propositional tautology or by propositional reasoning from other
formulae, we may write “PC” (standing for short ‘Propositional Calculus’).
Similarly, we provide any useful piece of information
justifying the derivation, such as “Ind. hypothesis”, “See …” or “Previously derived”.
In the example,
we use the rule
1
, which together with the rule
1
states that the connectives

\ast
and

(

holds whenever a heap

can be split into two subheaps whose domains have less than
\beta_{1}{+}1
and
\beta_{2}{+}1
elements, respectively.
We also use the following theorems and rules:

(

\psi\land\varphi\Rightarrow\varphi

\lnot\lnot

\ast
-Ilr

\text{}\frac{\displaystyle\varphi\Rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\quad\psi\Rightarrow\psi^{\prime}}{\displaystyle\varphi\ast\psi\Rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\ast\psi^{\prime}}{}

Main ingredients of the method.

Before showing completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
, let us recall the key ingredients of the method we follow,
not only to provide
a vade mecum for axiomatising other separation logics (which, in the second part of
[
DLM20
]
, we illustrate on the newly
introduced logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\exists{:}{\rightsquigarrow}\mathsf{)}
), but also to identify the
essential features and where variations are still possible.
The Hilbert-style axiomatisation of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
shall culminate
with Theorem
16
that states the adequateness of the proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
.

In order to axiomatise
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
internally, as already emphasised several times, the core formulae play an essential
role. The main properties of these formulae is that their Boolean combinations capture the full logic
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}

[
Loz04a
]

and all the core formulae can be expressed in
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
.
Generally speaking, our axiom system naturally leads to a form of constructive completeness, as
advocated in
[
Dou17
,
Lüc18
]
: the axiomatisation provides
proof-theoretical means to transform any formula into an equivalent Boolean combination
of core formulae, and it contains also a part dedicated to the derivation of valid Boolean combinations
of core formulae (understood as a syntactical fragment of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
).
What is specific to each logic is the design of the set of core formulae and in the case of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
,
this was already known since
[
Loz04a
]
.

Derivations in the proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
shall simulate the bottom-up elimination of
separating connectives (see forthcoming Lemmata
10
and
13
)
when the arguments are two Boolean combinations of core formulae.
To do so,
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
contains axiom schemas that perform such an elimination in multiple “small-step”
derivations, e.g. by deriving a single
\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
predicate from
\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\ast\top

(with forthcoming intermediate axiom
(

I
∗

17

)
).
Alternatively, it would have been possible to include “big-step” axiom schemas that, given the two Boolean combinations of
core formulae, derive the equivalent formula in one single derivation step (see e.g.
[
EIP19
]
).
The main difference is that small-step axioms provide
a simpler understanding of the key properties of the logic.

4.
A simple calculus for the core formulae

To axiomatise
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
, we start by introducing the proof
system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
dedicated to Boolean combinations of core formulae, see Figure
4
.
As explained earlier, it also contains the axiom schemata and modus ponens for the propositional calculus.
Moreover, the axioms whose name is of the form
A^{\mathsf{C}}_{i}
are axioms that remain in the global system for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
,
whereas those named
I^{\mathsf{C}}_{i}
are intermediate axioms that are removed when considering the axioms dealing with
the separating connectives. As explained before,
the intermediate axioms are handy to establish results about the axiomatisation of Boolean combinations of core
formulae but are not needed when all the axioms and rules of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*})
are considered.

In the axiom
(

A
C

3

)
,

\varphi{[\mathtt{y}{\leftarrow}\mathtt{x}]}
stands for
the formula obtained from
\varphi
by replacing with the variable
\mathtt{x}
every occurrence of
\mathtt{y}
.
Let
(s,h)
be a memory state.
The axioms state that
=
is an equivalence relation (first two axioms),

h(s(\mathtt{x}))=s(\mathtt{y})
implies
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h)
(axiom
(

A
C

5

)
) and
that
h
is a (partial) function (axiom
(

A
C

7

)
).
Furthermore, there are two intermediate axioms about size formulae:
(

I
C

9

)
states that if
{\rm dom}(h)
has at least
\beta{+}1
elements,
then it has at least
\beta
elements,
whereas
(

I
C

11

)
states instead that if there are
\beta
distinct memory cells corresponding to program variables, then indeed
{\rm dom}(h)\geq\beta
.
It is easy to check that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
is sound (see also Lemma
1
).
In order to establish its completeness with respect to
Boolean combinations of core formulae,
we first show that

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
is
complete for a subclass of Boolean combinations of core formulae, namely for
core types
defined below.
Then, we show that every formula in
\mathsf{Bool}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha))
is provably equivalent to a
disjunction of core types (Lemma
4
).

(
\mathbf{A^{\mathsf{C}}_{1}}
)

\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{x}

(
\mathbf{A^{\mathsf{C}}_{3}}
)

\varphi\land\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}\Rightarrow\varphi{[\mathtt{y}{\leftarrow}\mathtt{x}]}

(
\mathbf{A^{\mathsf{C}}_{5}}
)

\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\Rightarrow\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})

(
\mathbf{A^{\mathsf{C}}_{7}}
)

\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\land\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{z}\Rightarrow\mathtt{y}=\mathtt{z}

(
\mathbf{I^{\mathsf{C}}_{9}}
)

\mathtt{size}\geq\beta{+}1\Rightarrow\mathtt{size}\geq\beta

(
\mathbf{I^{\mathsf{C}}_{11}}
)

\bigwedge_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathtt{X}}(\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\bigwedge_{\mathtt{y}\in\mathtt{X}\setminus\{\mathtt{x}\}}\mathtt{x}\neq\mathtt{y})\Rightarrow\mathtt{size}\geq{\rm card}(\mathtt{X})

Figure 4.
Proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
for Boolean combinations of core formulae.

Introduction to core types.

Let
\mathtt{X}{\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}}\mathsf{VAR}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
We write
\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
to denote the set of
core types
defined by

{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left\{\varphi\in\mathsf{Conj}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha))\,\middle|\,{\scalebox{1}{${{{\begin{array}[]{r}{\rm for\ all}\ \psi\in\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha),\ \{\psi\mathbin{|}\lnot\psi\}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi,\text{ and }(\psi\land\lnot\psi)\not\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi\end{array}}}}$}}\right\}$}}.

Note that if
\varphi\in\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
, then
\varphi
is a conjunction such that
for every
\psi\in\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
, there is exactly one literal in
\varphi
built
upon
\psi
.

Lemma 3

(Refutational completeness)
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
, where
\alpha\geq{\rm card}(\mathtt{X})
.
The formula
\neg\varphi
is valid if and only if
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\neg\varphi
.

Proof 4.1
.

We show that
\varphi
is unsatisfiable if and only if
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\lnot\varphi
.
The “only if” part follows from the soundness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
, so we prove the “if” part.
Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
be such that
\not\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi\Rightarrow\bot
, and let us prove that

\varphi
is satisfiable.
By the axioms
(

A
C

1

)

and
(

A
C

3

)
,
there is an equivalence relation
\approx
on
\mathtt{X}
such that

\mathtt{x}\approx\mathtt{y}
iff
\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}
occurs positively
in
\varphi
. We write
[\mathtt{x}]
to denote the equivalence class of
\mathtt{x}

with respect to
\approx
.
By the axioms
(

A
C

3

)
and
(

A
C

7

)
,
there is a partial map
f:(\mathtt{X}/\approx)\to(\mathtt{X}/\approx)
on equivalence classes such
that
\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
occurs positively iff
f([x])
is defined and
f([x])=[y]
.
Let
D=\{[\mathtt{x}]\mid\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\mbox{ occurs positively in }\varphi\}
.
By the axiom
(

A
C

5

)
,
\mathsf{dom}(f)\subseteq D
.
Let
n=\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)
. We recall that, by definition of
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(.)
,

n
is the greatest
\beta
such that
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta
occurs positively in
\varphi

(or zero if there are none).

Let us show that
n\geq{\rm card}(D)
. Ad absurdum, suppose that
n<{\rm card}(D)
.
From the axiom
(

I
C

11

)
,
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi\Rightarrow\mathtt{size}\geq{\rm card}(D)

and by definition of
n
and the fact that
\alpha\geq{\rm card}(\mathtt{X})\geq{\rm card}(D)
,

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi\Rightarrow\mathtt{size}\geq n
and

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi\Rightarrow\neg(\mathtt{size}\geq(n+1))
since both
\mathtt{size}\geq n
and

(\mathtt{size}\geq(n+1))
(possibly negated) occur in
\varphi
as
\alpha\geq{\rm card}(\mathtt{X})
.
By using the axiom
(

I
C

9

)
and propositional reasoning,
we can get that
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi\Rightarrow\neg(\mathtt{size}\geq{\rm card}(D))
since

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi\Rightarrow\neg(\mathtt{size}\geq(n+1))
,
which leads to a contradiction. Consequently,
n\geq{\rm card}(D)
.

Let
\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\in\mathsf{LOC}
be
n+1
distinct locations, and let
us fix an enumeration
C_{1},\dots,C_{{\rm card}(D)}
on the equivalence classes of
\approx
.
Let
(s,h)
be defined by

•

s(\mathtt{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\ell_{i}
if
[x]
is the
i
th equivalence class
C_{i}
,

•

h(\ell_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\ell_{j}
if
0<i\leq{\rm card}(D)
and the
i
th equivalence class is mapped to the
j
th
one by
f
,

•

h(\ell_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\ell_{0}
if either
0<i\leq{\rm card}(D)
and the
i
th equivalence class is not in the domain of
f
, or
i>{\rm card}(D)
.

Then, by construction,
(s,h)
satisfies all positive literals of the form
\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}
or

\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}
or
\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})

that occur positively in
\varphi
, and all negative literals that occur in
\varphi
. It also
satisfies
\mathtt{size}\geq n
, falsifies
\mathtt{size}\geq n+1
(assuming
n+1\leq\alpha
), and by the axiom
(

I
C

9

)
, it satisfies all size literals
in
\varphi
.

By classical reasoning, one can show that every
\varphi\in\mathsf{Bool}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha))

is provably equivalent to a disjunction of core types. Together with Lemma
3
, this implies that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
is adequate with respect to the propositional logic of core formulae.

To prove forthcoming Theorem
5
, let us first establish the following simple lemma.

Lemma 4

(Core Types Lemma)
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{Bool}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha))
. There is a disjunction

\psi=\psi_{1}\vee\ldots\vee\psi_{n}
with

\psi_{i}\in\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\max({\rm card}(\mathtt{X}),\alpha))
for all
i

such that
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi\Leftrightarrow\psi
.

Proof 4.2
.

Let
\psi_{1}\vee\ldots\vee\psi_{n}
be a formula in disjunctive normal form logically equivalent to
\varphi
.
If
\psi_{i}
is not a core type in
\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\max({\rm card}(\mathtt{X}),\alpha))
,
there is a core formula

\chi\in\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\max({\rm card}(\mathtt{X}),\alpha))

that occurs neither positively nor negatively in
\psi_{i}
.
Replacing
\psi_{i}
with
(\psi_{i}\wedge\chi)\vee(\psi_{i}\wedge\neg\chi)
,
and repeating this for all missing core formulae and for all
i
, we obtain a
disjunction of core types of the expected form. Since all equivalences follow from pure propositional reasoning, the equivalence between
\varphi
and the obtained formula can be proved
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
.

Theorem 5

(Adequacy)
.

A Boolean combination of core formulae
\varphi
is valid iff
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi
.

Proof 4.3
.

Let
\varphi
be a Boolean combination of core formulae in
\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
for some
\mathtt{X}
and
\alpha
.
As all the axioms are valid (Lemma
1
),

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi
implies that
\varphi
is
valid.
Let us assume that
\varphi
is valid, and let us prove that
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi
.
By Lemma
4
, there is a disjunction
\psi=\psi_{1}\vee\ldots\vee\psi_{n}
of core types
in
\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\max({\rm card}(\mathtt{X}),\alpha))

such that
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}(\neg\varphi)\Leftrightarrow\psi
.
As
\varphi
is valid, the formulae

\neg\varphi
,
\psi
and all the
\psi_{i}
’s are unsatisfiable.
By Lemma
3
,
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\psi_{i}\Rightarrow\bot
, for all
i
.
By propositional reasoning,
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}\varphi
.

5.
Axiomatisation for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}

We write
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}
to denote the fragment of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
in which the separating implication is removed
at the price of adding the atomic formulae of the form
\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.
We define an Hilbert-style axiomatisation for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}
,
obtained by enriching
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
with axioms and one inference rule
that handle the separating conjunction
\ast
, leading to the proof system
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
.
Fundamentally, as we work now within
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}
, the core formula
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta
can be encoded in the logic.
According to its definition,
given in Section
2.2
,
we see
\mathtt{size}\geq 0
as
\top
,
\mathtt{size}\geq 1
as
\lnot\mathtt{emp}
and

Figure 5.
Additional axioms and rule for
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
.

The axioms and the rule added to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
in order to define
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
are presented in Figure
5
.
Their soundness has been proved in Lemma
1
,
with the exception of the three intermediate axioms

(

I
∗

11

)
,

(

I
∗

13

)

and
(

I
∗

17

)
,
which are used for the completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
with respect to
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}
,
but are discharged from the proof system for
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathbin{-\mkern-6.0mu*}\mathsf{)}
(Figure
1
), as they become derivable (Lemma
12
).

Lemma 6
.

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
is sound.

Proof 5.1
.

The axioms
(

I
∗

11

)

and
(

I
∗

13

)
are inherited from Boolean BI (see
[
BV14
]
and
[
GLW06
, Section 2]
). The soundness of
(

I
∗

17

)
is straightforward.
Indeed, suppose
(s,h)\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\ast\top
.
So, there is
h^{\prime}{\sqsubseteq}h
such that
(s,h^{\prime})\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
. By definition of
\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
,
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h^{\prime})
.
By
h^{\prime}{\sqsubseteq}h
,
s(\mathtt{x})\in{\rm dom}(h)
. We conclude that

(s,h)\models\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
.

Let us look further at the axioms in Figure
5
.
The axioms deal with the commutative monoid properties of
(\ast,\mathtt{emp})
and its distributivity
over
\vee
(as for Boolean BI, see e.g.
[
GLW06
]
).
The rule

∗

-Intro:
,
sometimes called “frame rule” by analogy with the
rule of the same name in program logic,
states that logical equivalence is a congruence
for
\ast
.

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
is
designed with the idea of being as simple as possible. On one side, this helps understanding the key ingredients of
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}
. On the other side, this makes the proof of completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
more challenging. To work towards this proof while familiarising with the new axioms, we first show a set of
intermediate theorems (see Appendix
A
).

Lemma 7
.

The following rules and axioms are admissible in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
:

(
\mathbf{I^{\ast}_{\ref{lemma:separate-auxiliary-stuff}.1}}
)

\mathtt{x}\sim\mathtt{y}\land(\varphi\ast\psi)\Rightarrow(\varphi\land\mathtt{x}\sim\mathtt{y})\ast\psi
,
where
\sim
stands for
=
or
\neq
.

(
\mathbf{I^{\ast}_{\ref{lemma:separate-auxiliary-stuff}.3}}
)

\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}\land((\varphi\land\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))\ast\psi)\Rightarrow(\varphi\land\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{y}))\ast\psi
.

(
\mathbf{I^{\ast}_{\ref{lemma:separate-auxiliary-stuff}.5}}
)

(\varphi\land\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))\ast\psi\Rightarrow\varphi\ast(\psi\land\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))
.

(
\mathbf{I^{\ast}_{\ref{lemma:separate-auxiliary-stuff}.7}}
)

\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land(\varphi\ast\psi)\Rightarrow(\varphi\land\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))\ast\psi
.

(
\mathbf{I^{\ast}_{\ref{lemma:separate-auxiliary-stuff}.9}}
)

\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land(\varphi\ast(\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\psi))\Rightarrow(\varphi\land\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))\ast(\lnot\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\land\psi)

(
\mathbf{I^{\ast}_{\ref{lemma:separate-auxiliary-stuff}.11}}
)

\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\land((\varphi\land\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}))\ast\psi)\Rightarrow(\varphi\land\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y})\ast\psi
.

(
\mathbf{I^{\ast}_{\ref{lemma:separate-auxiliary-stuff}.13}}
)

\lnot\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y}\land(\varphi\ast\psi)\Rightarrow(\varphi\land\lnot\mathtt{x}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{y})\ast\psi
.

\begin{aligned} &\bigwedge{\scalebox{0.8}{$\left\{\mathtt{x}\sim\mathtt{y}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\{\varphi\mathbin{|}\psi\}\,\middle|\,{{{\begin{array}[]{r}\sim\in\{=,\neq\}\end{array}}}}\right\}$}}&\land&\bigwedge{\scalebox{0.8}{$\left\{\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\{\varphi\mathbin{|}\psi\}\right\}$}}\\

\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{2}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi\end{array}}}}\right\}$}}\end{aligned}

Figure 6.
The formula
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.

From now on, we understand
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
as the proof system obtained from
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}
by adding all schemata from
Figure
5
but by removing
(

I
C

9

)
and
(

I
C

11

)
.
We show that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
enjoys the
\ast
elimination
property when the argument formulae are core types. That is, given two satisfiable core types
\varphi
and
\psi
, in
\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
, we show that the formula
\varphi\ast\psi
is provably equivalent to
the formula

{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
in
\mathsf{Conj}(\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},2\alpha))
, defined in Figure
6
.

Lemma 9
.

Let
\mathtt{X}\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{VAR}
and
\alpha\geq{\rm card}(\mathtt{X})
.
If
\varphi
and
\psi
are two satisfiable core types in

\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
, then

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}\varphi\ast\psi\Leftrightarrow{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.

The equivalence
\varphi\ast\psi\Leftrightarrow{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)

is reminiscent to the one in
[
EIP19
, Lemma 3]
that is proved semantically.
In a way, because
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
will reveal to be complete, the restriction of

[
EIP19
, Lemma 3]
to
\mathsf{SL(}\ast,\mathtt{alloc}\mathsf{)}
can be
replayed completely syntactically within
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast){}
.

Proof 5.5

(Structure of the proof of Lemma
9
)
.

Before presenting the technical developments, let us explain the structure of the whole proof of Lemma
9
, which might help
to follow the different steps. In order to show that

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}\varphi\ast\psi\Leftrightarrow{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
,
we start showing that
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}\varphi\ast\psi\Rightarrow{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.
This can be done rather mechanically since for every literal

L
of
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
, one can construct a derivation for

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}\varphi\ast\psi\Rightarrow L
.
The main difficulty in the proof rests on showing that

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\varphi\ast\psi
.
To do so, we build a sequence of
formulae
\varphi^{(1)}\!\ast\psi^{(1)}
,

\varphi^{(2)}\!\ast\psi^{(2)}
,
\dots
,

\varphi^{(k)}\!\ast\psi^{(k)}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

for all
i\in[1,k]
,

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\varphi^{(i)}\ast\psi^{(i)}
,
the formulae
\varphi^{(i)}
and
\psi^{(i)}
are conjunctions of core formulae, and

•

for all
j\in[1,i]
,
\varphi^{(j)}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi^{(i)}
and

\psi^{(j)}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi^{(i)}
.

•

\varphi=\varphi^{(k)}
and
\psi=\psi^{(k)}

(modulo associativity/commutativity of the classical conjunction).

In order to build
\varphi^{i+1}
(resp.
\psi^{i+1}
), we identify a literal
L

in
\varphi
(resp. in
\psi
) that does not occur yet in
\varphi^{i}

and we show that
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\varphi^{(i+1)}\ast\psi^{(i+1)}
with
\varphi^{i+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\varphi^{(i)}\wedge L

(resp.
\psi^{(i+1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\psi^{(i)}\wedge L
)
and
\psi^{(i+1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\psi^{(i)}
(resp.
\varphi^{(i+1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\varphi^{(i)}
).
The case analysis on the shape of the literal
L
is rather mechanical but it remains to specify
how the first formulae
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\psi^{(1)}
are designed.
In short,
\varphi^{(1)}
(resp.
\psi^{(1)}
) is dedicated to the
part of
\varphi
(resp.
\psi
)
related to the size of the heap domain and to the allocated variables. Details
will follow.

To construct these above-mentioned derivations, some additional derivations are instrumental
in particular to establish that the formulae below are derivable in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)
:

\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\Rightarrow\mathtt{size}=\beta_{1}\ast\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{2},\ \ \ \ \ \ \mathtt{size}=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\Rightarrow\mathtt{size}=\beta_{1}\ast\mathtt{size}=\beta_{2}.

Such derivations can be found in Appendix
B
.
We now develop the proof of Lemma
9
.

Proof 5.6

(Proof of Lemma

)
.

First of all, let us briefly explain what is the rationale for having literals of the form
\mathtt{x}\neq\mathtt{x}

in the definition of
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.
Recall that
{\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\{\varphi\mathbin{;}\psi\}}

\varphi
and

\psi

Let us show that
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\varphi\ast\psi

If

(Theorem

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\perp

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\varphi\ast\psi

Since
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\varphi\ast\psi

Otherwise, below, we assume
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
to be satisfiable.
In particular, this implies that no literals of the form
{\mathtt{x}\neq\mathtt{x}}

{\neg\mathtt{size}\geq 0}

{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)

\varphi

{\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{y}}
,
i.e.
\varphi
,

contain exactly

Since
\varphi
is satisfiable, these equalities
define an equivalence relation.
Let
\mathtt{x}_{1},\ldots\mathtt{x}_{n}
be a maximal enumeration of representatives of the

\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x}_{i})
occurs in
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.

\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
there is

\mathtt{x}_{i}
is syntactically equal to
\mathtt{x}
.
Consequently, from the definition of
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
,

\mathtt{alloc}(\mathtt{x})
occurs in
\varphi
or in
\psi
, then
there is some
\mathtt{x}_{i}
such that
\mathtt{x}=\mathtt{x}_{i}
occurs in
\varphi
(and therefore also in

\psi
and in
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
).

1

After deriving

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\textit{\small{Alloc}}\ast\top
and

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}\textit{\small{Alloc}}\Rightarrow\mathtt{size}=n
,
the proof is divided in three steps: (1) we isolate the allocated cells and the garbage, (2) we distribute the alloc and size literals according to the goal
\varphi\ast\psi
and (3) we add the missing literals.

Step 1, isolating allocated cells and garbage

Since
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
is a conjunction of literals built from core formulae,
we can rely on
\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))
,
i.e. the maximum
\beta
among the formulae
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta
appearing positively in
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
. First, we show some important properties of
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
, related to
\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))
.

A
.

\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))=\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)
,

B
.

If there is
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
, then

\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+1\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\varphi}
,

\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)+1\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\psi}
.

C
.

If there is
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
, then

\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))+1\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.

Proof 5.7

(Proof of
A.
)
.

By definition of
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(.)
, we know that
\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
and
{\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi
.
By definition of
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
,

\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.

Ad absurdum
, suppose that
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)\neq\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))
and thus, by definition of
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(.)
, there is
\beta>\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)
such that
{\mathtt{size}\geq\beta}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.
By definition of
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
,
we conclude that there are
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
such that

\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta
,

\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{1}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
and
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{2}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi
.
As
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}>\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)
,
either
\beta_{1}>\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)
or
\beta_{2}>\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)
.
Let us assume
\beta_{1}>\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)

(the other case is analogous).
We have
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{1}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
.
However, this is contradictory,
since by definition of
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(.)
for all
\beta^{\prime}>\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)
,

\mathtt{size}\geq\beta^{\prime}\not\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
.
Thus,
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)=\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))
.

Proof 5.8

(Proof of
B.
)
.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)}
.
By definition of
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
, this implies
that there are
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in[0,\alpha]

such that
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}1
,
\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{1}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
and
\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{2}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi
.
Since
\varphi
and
\psi
are satisfiable,
by definition of
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(.)
, we derive that

\beta_{1}>\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)
and
\beta_{2}>\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)
.
This implies that the core formula
\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+1
belongs to
\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
and, analogously,
that the core formula
\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)+1
belongs to
\mathsf{Core}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
.
Since
\varphi
is in
\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
,
this implies that
\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+1
is an atomic formula appearing in
\varphi
. By definition of
\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)
, the formula cannot appear positively,
i.e.
\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\varphi)+1\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi
.
Analogously,
\psi
is in
\mathsf{CoreTypes}(\mathtt{X},\alpha)
,
which leads to
\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\max_{\mathtt{size}}(\psi)+1\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi
.

Proof 5.9

(Proof of
C.
)
.

Directly from
A.
and
B.
.
Indeed, by definition of
{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
,
we know that
for every
{\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\varphi}
and every
\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta^{\prime}\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}\psi
,

\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta+\beta^{\prime}\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}1\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)
.

Now, let us consider
{\beta_{g}=\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}n}
.
We define the formula
Garb
below:

\textit{\small{Garb}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\mathtt{size}=\beta_{g}&\text{if}~\lnot\mathtt{size}\geq\beta\subseteq_{\mathsf{Lt}}{{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)},~\text{for some}~\beta\\
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{g}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where we recall that
\mathtt{size}=\beta_{g}

stands for
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{g}\land\lnot(\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{g}+1)
.
Notice that
Garb
is a conjunction of literals where at least one
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta
occurs positively (i.e.
\mathtt{size}\geq 0
).
The objective of this step of the proof is to show that
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\textit{\small{Alloc}}\ast\textit{\small{Garb}}
.
First, we focus on the positive part of
Garb
, and prove

{\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow\textit{\small{Alloc}}\ast\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{g}}
.
If
{\beta_{g}=0}
then
\mathtt{size}\geq\beta_{g}=\top
and we have already shown

\vdash_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{C}}(\ast)}{\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi)\Rightarrow{\textit{\small{Alloc}}\ast\top}
.
So, let us assume that
\beta_{g}>1
. Notice that then

\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}n=\max_{\mathtt{size}}({\langle\mbox{$\ast$}\rangle}(\varphi,\psi))-n
.
